# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-27

| **[SceneBloom: Agentic Indoor Environment Generation with Realistic and Diverse Layouts for Embodied Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32978v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training generalizable embodied agents requires realistic, varied, and simulation-ready indoor environments. Language-guided methods support semantic planning and object-level reasoning, but precise spatial grounding remains difficult. Visual generative models offer rich priors over plausible arrangements, yet their outputs require geometric reasoning to become physically valid 3D scenes. We introduce SceneBloom, an agentic framework that combines visual layout sampling with contract-guided 3D compilation. A hierarchical sampler uses diffusion priors to generate furniture arrangements within functional zones. A compiler then uses relations extracted from each proposal to guide geometric fitting and local pose correction. Across five room types, SceneBloom attains the highest visual quality and physical stability among the compared methods and is the only method with no detected mesh collisions and out-of-bounds objects. Its layouts are also closer to the distribution of real rooms than those of all compared baselines. Object navigation policies trained in these realistic scenes achieve a higher success rate in held-out real scans than those trained on an equal number of real training scans. Code is available at https://github.com/Tinaliu0123/scenebloom.</p></details> |  |
| **[GaP: A Graph-as-Policy Multi-Agent Self-Learning Harness For Variational Automation Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05369v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For robots to work reliably in commercial and industrial applications, recent advances in agentic coding systems have potential to combine interpretable robot programming with the open-world adaptability of model-free policies. We focus on "Variational Automation" (VA), a class of tasks that have larger variations in object geometry and pose than fixed automation. Model-free policies often struggle to close the reliability gap for VA tasks, which must be executed persistently and reliably in commercial and industrial applications. Motivated by prior work on Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) and the Robot Operating System (ROS), we introduce Graph-as-Policy (GaP), a multi-agent coding harness that generates directed computation graphs with perception, planning, and control nodes from a Modular Open Robot Skill Library (MORSL). GaP generates an internal simulation environment to rehearse task instances with different graphs in parallel to iteratively refine the graph structure and parameters to improve success rates and throughput. Evaluation with 8 new open VA task benchmarks, 4 in simulation and 4 in real-world, suggests that GaP can achieve success rates that significantly outperform baselines. Details, code, and data: https://graph-robots.github.io/gap</p></details> |  |
| **[VETTA: Coordinating Turn- and Token-Level Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08402v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn LLM agents often receive sparse task feedback across several interactions, while generating each response token by token. This creates two related credit-assignment questions: which responses helped achieve the outcome, and which generation decisions mattered within each response? Existing methods typically focus on only one level: turn-level methods evaluate complete responses but do not distinguish the decisions within them; token-level methods can propagate feedback across turns but do not explicitly model credit for each response. These complementary limitations motivate learning credit at both levels and coordinating it in a single policy update. We introduce VETTA, a credit assignment method that jointly learns turn- and token-level values through separate heads on a shared lightweight critic. VETTA computes advantages along both temporal sequences and combines each turn advantage with a within-response-centered token residual for PPO updates. Furthermore, to reduce value-learning cost, the critic retains only early Transformer blocks from the pretrained checkpoint used to initialize the actor. Experiments on four agent benchmarks demonstrate VETTA's strong performance. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, VETTA achieves success rates of 95.5% on ALFWorld, 76.0% on WebShop, and 52.2% on ScienceWorld. With Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct, it reaches 84.9% and 80.7% success on Sokoban under sparse and dense rewards, respectively. Critic-depth comparisons further show strong task performance with substantially lower critic-side computation. These results demonstrate that coordinating turn- and token-level credit with a lightweight shared critic improves agent performance while keeping value estimation efficient. Code is available at https://github.com/Jiaju-Chen/VETTA-official.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 6 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Muse Glimmer 30B: An On-Device Agentic Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24628v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce Muse Glimmer, an open-source 30B parameter causal language model with perception encoder, purpose-built for autonomous agentic tasks on consumer hardware. Muse Glimmer integrates multi-step reasoning, reliable tool use and failure recovery into a single model that runs locally on consumer devices without requiring cloud infrastructure. The model accepts text and image inputs and produces text outputs, was trained on data from over a hundred languages, and is quantized to approximately 4-bit precision for efficient single-GPU inference. Muse Glimmer achieves state-of-the-art performance for its size class on agentic benchmarks, setting a new standard compared to the models of its size: Gemma4-31B and Qwen3.6-27B on tool use, multi-step planning, and agentic task completion. We release the model under the Apache 2.0 license to foster open-source development of capable, safe, local AI agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[Do "True" and "False" Add Up to One? Complement Consistency in LLM-Agent Opinion Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32874v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In LLM-agent opinion simulations, each agent reports the probability that a claim is true after seeing its own previous probability and the probabilities reported by its neighbors, and that number becomes its state in the next round. If the number describes the agent's position rather than the sentence it was shown, then rewriting the claim as its complement and replacing every probability p with 1 - p should return the complementary answer, so the two reports should sum to one. We test this with three small open models: Qwen3-4B, Llama-3.2-3B and Gemma-3-4B. On 2,304 agent states taken from a running network simulation, Qwen's paired reports sum to 1.18 on average, and on 48 real fact-checking claims from AVeriTeC they again exceed one. A controlled benchmark of 24 worlds, each with exactly two possible outcomes, shows the same failure in all three models. The discrepancy is two to ten times larger than the change produced by a same-meaning paraphrase, and it persists under repeated sampling and across peer group sizes and report strengths. When the reports are fed back into two populations that start from complementary states, the populations do not remain mirror images: their divergence is positive after five rounds for every model. Averaging each answer with the complement of its paired answer restores consistency by construction and lowers Brier loss averaged over the two wordings, but at the final round it beats the better of the two original wordings for only one of the three models. A schedule-by-targeting experiment on the same model gives a small, stable interaction because such sweeps keep the claim wording fixed. Consistency under rewording therefore needs its own test before simulated probabilities are read as beliefs</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentGUI: An Interface for Observing and Steering Long-Running AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26300v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents are increasingly adept at tackling complex, long-running tasks. With the rapid surge of autonomous capabilities, human oversight is systematically lagging behind due to limited human-centered interfacing. Aiming to address this, we introduce AgentGUI, a user-friendly, locally hosted GUI for seamlessly observing and steering AI agents amid multiple concurrent, long-running sessions. AgentGUI features 1) rich agent trajectory visualizations, 2) effective manual and automated steering, and 3) integration with and coordination between open-source and frontier agent frameworks. A controlled user study demonstrates statistically significant reduction in the time it takes to identify key elements from agent traces (38% faster, p = 0.023). In a preliminary experiment, AgentGUI's automated drift prevention feature raises the task completion rate of small local agents by as high as 34pp across a 0.8B--9B model ladder (N=50 runs per model). AgentGUI is publicly available through its project website (https://agent-gui-project.github.io) and open-source repository (https://github.com/eth-medical-ai-lab/agent-gui), along with a demo video (https://youtube.com/watch?v=GSDyxN1gTF0).</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 System Demonstrations</p></details> |
| **[Reimagining Enterprise RAG for Multi-Agent Systems: A Knowledge Layer That Organizes Before It Retrieves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32851v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprises are deploying agentic AI across the software development lifecycle, and they need the behavioral logic of the systems they act on, not stakeholder documentation. A seven-agent reverse-engineering pipeline recovered this logic, processing 241 repositories across 18 services into thousands of artifacts describing scope, journeys, rules and acceptance criteria. Recovery does not confer usability. Entities like offer, eligibility, and activation recur across services under a shared vocabulary, related in name but operationally distinct, so one end-to-end journey implicates over a thousand near-identical rules that must not be conflated. Flat vector retrieval fails, returning an identical rule from the wrong service at high similarity with no signal of error. Content-level ontology, the traditional remedy, did not exist, and relating every occurrence of recurring entities would have cost more than the engagement and not survived a release cycle. We place the ontology at the artifact level. In this machine-generated corpus, each artifact retains its producer, component, service, domain and type, provenance that disambiguates before content is read. We present a knowledge layer that organizes before retrieving: a self-describing catalog, a sparse metadata registry, and a navigation hypergraph defined over artifacts rather than extracted entities. Queries resolve to artifact identifiers that constrain vector search, so the competing rule from another service is absent rather than outranked, making disambiguation structural, not statistical. Built in under a week and admitting new services by convention, the layer serves SDLC agents, developers, quality engineers and analysts. It supported root-cause analysis and resolution of 100+ production defects with developer verification and attributed 35 release regression failures to specific rules and services.</p></details> |  |
| **[Frontier-OPD: Discovering the Edge of Agent Capability and Teaching Agents to Go Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32802v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>On-policy distillation aligns teacher feedback with a student's current behavior, yet acquiring useful supervision for multi-turn agents requires identifying both where guidance is needed and whether the student can sustain the interaction afterward. We introduce Frontier-OPD, which addresses this problem through the student's learning frontier: decision situations that remain challenging but admit useful autonomous continuation after brief teacher guidance. To operationalize this frontier, Frontier-OPD organizes on-policy experience into a shared-state trajectory graph, where trajectory-balanced aggregation of teacher-student disagreement identifies distinct intervention candidates while preserving the histories needed for replay. Short teacher bridges are then assessed by comparing agreement over the bridge and the ensuing student continuation with an equally long segment of the original trajectory, providing a behavioral proxy for guidance that the current student can build on. Accepted teacher responses augment ordinary distillation data, and fresh rollouts renew the frontier estimate as the policy changes. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld report higher mean autonomous performance than the compared distillation baselines across the evaluated student scales and teacher configurations, with ablations indicating complementary effects of candidate selection and guidance filtering. In one ALFWorld timing setting, the additional collection cost is offset by a shorter time to a target success rate.</p></details> |  |
| **[AFRA: Age-Threshold Frame-Based Random Access for Timely Communication in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32785v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Timely state updates are essential for coordination and monitoring in multi-agent systems, such as teams of mobile robots exchanging state information. When agents share a wireless channel, collisions and slow convergence of distributed access schemes can degrade information freshness over finite operating periods. This paper proposes age-threshold frame-based random access (AFRA), a distributed medium access control protocol for minimizing age of information (AoI). Once its age reaches a threshold, an agent starts a local frame with a mandatory transmission and, if unsuccessful, makes one further attempt at a uniformly selected remaining slot. Each attempt carries a freshly generated update. AFRA requires only local state and acknowledgment feedback, with shared slot boundaries but independently timed local frames. We prove almost-sure convergence from any legal initial state to collision-free periodic operation when the frame length $L$ and age threshold $T$ satisfy $L\ge T>N\ge2$, where $N$ is the number of agents. We also derive closed-form expressions for steady-state throughput, average AoI, and average peak AoI. Simulations demonstrate that AFRA combines rapid convergence at small age thresholds with lower window-averaged AoI than the existing age-aware random access schemes evaluated in this study.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for presentation at IEEE ROBIO 2026</p></details> |
| **[Every Edge Needs a Quote: Building a Quote-Grounded Knowledge Graph of Healthcare Software Adoption with Language-Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32781v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hospitals regularly buy software that clinicians and patients depend on. Because these purchases are costly, hard to reverse and can affect patient care, buyers look for evidence of which peer institutions already use a product and which systems it connects to. This evidence is public but scattered across vendor websites, press releases and third-party pages. Language-model agents can now collect it at low cost, but the correctness of the extracted claims is difficult to establish, and a wrongly listed customer can mislead buyers and misrepresent an organisation. We present a pipeline that builds a knowledge graph of customer and integration relations from the open web under one rule: every edge must be supported by a verbatim quote from its source page. Pages come from a deterministic crawl of each vendor's site and a budgeted ReAct agent that searches the rest of the web. A permissive extractor proposes relations with quotes, and a layered grounding stage decides which to keep, using a referent step that reads the whole page when a quote names only one side, deterministic checks, and a panel of five model calls voting on nine criteria. On 61 vendors, the pipeline reaches 0.94 precision and 0.71 recall against a page-level key covering the 6,428 candidate relations proposed by its extractor, whose verdicts were drafted by one model and re-checked with a second. Against the golden reference, the marketplace's own curated list, it scores lower, but 89% of the relations it keeps that the reference lacks are stated on a page. A confidence score built from the panel's votes keeps more correct relations than the fixed rule at the same precision, and reaches 0.90 precision at 0.90 recall. We also discuss how narrow, bounded model calls, string-matched evidence and voting address long-context degradation and run-to-run variance.</p></details> | <details><summary>19 pa...</summary><p>19 pages, 3 figures, 9 tables. Code: https://github.com/MEDomicsLab/healthcare-agentic-kg</p></details> |
| **[A-MapReduce: Executing Wide Search via Agentic MapReduce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01331v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Contemporary large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems exhibit systematic advantages in deep research tasks, which emphasize iterative, vertically structured information seeking. However, when confronted with wide search tasks characterized by large-scale, breadth-oriented retrieval, existing agentic frameworks, primarily designed around sequential, vertically structured reasoning, remain stuck in expansive search objectives and inefficient long-horizon execution. To bridge this gap, we propose A-MapReduce, a MapReduce paradigm-inspired multi-agent execution framework that recasts wide search as a horizontally structured retrieval problem. Concretely, A-MapReduce implements parallel processing of massive retrieval targets through task-adaptive decomposition and structured result aggregation. Meanwhile, it leverages experiential memory to drive the continual evolution of query-conditioned task allocation and recomposition, enabling progressive improvement in large-scale wide-search regimes. Extensive experiments on five agentic benchmarks demonstrate that A-MapReduce is (i) high-performing, achieving state-of-the-art performance on WideSearch and DeepWideSearch, and delivering 5.11% - 17.50% average Item F1 improvements compared with strong baselines with OpenAI o3 or Gemini 2.5 Pro backbones; (ii) cost-effective and efficient, delivering superior cost-performance trade-offs and reducing running time by 45.8\% compared to representative multi-agent baselines. The code is available at https://github.com/mingju-c/AMapReduce.</p></details> | 27 pages |
| **[PAIR-SAFE: A Paired-Agent Approach for Runtime Auditing and Refining AI-Mediated Mental Health Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.12754v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs are increasingly used for mental health support, yet they can produce responses that are overly directive, inconsistent, or clinically misaligned. Most risk mitigation approaches rely on implicit alignment through training or prompting, offering limited runtime accountability. We introduce PAIR-SAFE, a paired-agent framework that integrates a Responder agent with a Judge agent grounded in the clinically validated Motivational Interviewing Treatment Integrity (MITI-4). The Judge audits each response and issues \allow{} or \revise{} decisions to guide real-time refinement. We evaluate interactions using a support-seeker simulator derived from human-annotated data. The Judge-supervised interactions show significant improvements in key MITI dimensions, including Partnership, Seek Collaboration, and overall Relational quality. Our quantitative findings are corroborated by expert evaluation, highlighting nuances of runtime supervision. Our study reveals that paired-agent auditing can provide clinically grounded, transparent oversight for AI-assisted mental health support.</p></details> |  |
| **[Semantic Representation for Agentic Self-Play in Automated Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32755v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Navigating complex, high-dimensional biological search spaces necessitates scalable physical and computational intelligence to explore beyond local optima through iterative self-play across ideation, experimentation, and automation. Translating experimental designs into reliable physical execution remains bottlenecked by static laboratory automation and unmodeled physical variability. To address this, we introduce VERified Infrastructure for Discovery Laboratories, or VERID, a foundational infrastructure designed to bridge the gap between the high-level reasoning of artificial intelligence (AI) agents and complex, error-prone physical realities of wet labs. VERID is a persistent, high-fidelity semantic digital twin of the laboratory workcell that provides four core services: queryable introspection for agentic reasoning, time-compressed simulation for protocol validation, comprehensive trace persistence for a complete scientific record, and a framework for safe execution. We present three case studies using aqueous dye and deionized water mixtures to validate the optical and fluidic foundations of VERID. These studies demonstrate how VERID enables pre-emptive detection of critical errors in experimental protocols, stateful mid-run error recovery and post-hoc identification of subtle physical failures. The simulation functions as a discrete accounting engine, allowing agents to safely and inexpensively explore experimental designs in silico and refine experimental strategies through iterative self-play. VERID also captures structured multimodal experimental data for downstream analysis, knowledge mining and agent training. We argue that this semantic layer is an essential prerequisite for deploying autonomous agents in the biochemical and biomedical sciences, ensuring data integrity, operational safety, and paving the way for tackling previously intractable grand challenges in biology.</p></details> | 16 pages, 4 figures |
| **[ActionRAG: Evidence-Refining Graph Diffusion for Long-Term Memory in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32752v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents need long-term memory that can recover precise and mutually consistent evidence from growing interaction histories, particularly when answers depend on relations among facts rather than isolated semantic matches. Current memory pipelines remain vulnerable at two stages: indexing errors can fragment repeated entities or merge distinct ones, and online retrieval often treats initial similarity scores as final even when the surrounding graph provides evidence for revising them. We introduce ActionRAG, a graph memory architecture that couples cautious indexing with query-time graph retroevaluation. Its indexing pipeline reconciles type labels and applies tiered entity resolution. Given a query, dual-channel entity and triple retrieval forms an initial evidence set and induces a bounded local graph. Query-conditioned receiver-normalized diffusion refines triple scores using local path structure. Seed protection, hub-penalized flux, and triple materialization preserve direct evidence while recovering missed connections. We evaluate ActionRAG on MemoryAgentBench (MAB), which tests accurate retrieval, conflict resolution, long-range understanding, and test-time learning. Across its eight deterministic-scoring columns, ActionRAG achieves 55.1 average accuracy, improving over the strongest retrieval-based memory baseline by 5.4 points. Under MAB's query-time cost accounting, mean synthesis cost stays below \$0.001 per query on the four reported cost columns, and the sum of separately measured retrieval-side p99 components is 440.7 ms on our consumer Apple Silicon setup.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agentic Hazard Mapper: Emulating Expert Map-Making with AI Agents for Risk Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32738v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hazard maps are essential tools for public risk communication, preparedness, and evacuation planning, yet effective cartographic design is not always ensured due to limited access to cartographic expertise across local governments and communities. We present Agentic Hazard Mapper, a multi-agent system that automates hazard-map production by emulating professional cartographic workflows and iterative design trade-offs while adapting design decisions to different data, locations, and hazard contexts. We conducted a formative study with map experts to inform the system design, followed by a user study and interviews with map experts and hazard practitioners. The generated maps improved participants' spatial understanding and evacuation-planning performance, reduced perceived workload and task completion time, and were generally preferred over corresponding municipal maps. We further discuss human oversight, adaptation across users and communication contexts, and opportunities to extend agentic support across broader stages of hazard-map production and risk communication.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Butterfly Defects: Tracing Cooperation in LLM Agent Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32726v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) increasingly operate as interconnected agents, a fundamental question emerges: does individual cooperation translate into collective cooperation? We investigate this question through the Iterated Prisoner's Dilemma (IPD), moving from dyadic interactions to homogeneous and heterogeneous networks of open-weight LLMs. Our findings reveal that cooperation is highly sensitive to model composition and seemingly minor linguistic changes. Models that cooperate with identical peers can shift toward defection in mixed populations. A simple uncertainty cue can increase or decrease cooperation, redistribute costs, and affect unexposed agents several hops away. Even reversing action-label order can dramatically alter behavior, with uncertainty framing sometimes amplifying this sensitivity. Together, these findings challenge the notion of cooperation as a stable model-level property, revealing how strategic behavior depends on interaction partners, network positions, and the language used to frame decisions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Temporal Fair Division in Multi-Agent Systems: From Precise Alternation Metrics to Scalable Coordination Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14879v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Many multi-agent games and simulated environments involve several independent, often learning, agents repeatedly competing for a shared resource. Conventional fairness measures assess whether resources are shared equally overall, but cannot distinguish orderly turn-taking from irregular access patterns that produce long, unpredictable waiting times despite similar cumulative outcomes. We introduce Rotational Periodicity, a family of computationally efficient metrics combining two complementary measurements, the regularity of waiting times between successful accesses and the balance of access frequencies across agents. We evaluate them against a more detailed family of alternation metrics in a repeated threshold-congestion game, the Honey-Jar Game, in which two to ten reinforcement-learning agents compete for exclusive access to a shared high-value resource. Our experiments show that independently trained Q-learning agents often coordinate substantially worse than random-policy agents, even though conventional fairness metrics consistently report favourable outcomes. Every member of the family closely reproduces the rankings of the more computationally expensive alternation metrics, while computing six to twenty times faster as the number of agents grows. A direct timing study, which separates the two quantities that drive that cost, measures the advantage growing in proportion to the number of agents out to two thousand agents. These findings show that evaluating multi-agent learning systems requires temporally aware measures of coordination, not only aggregate outcomes, and that efficient proxies make this practical for larger multi-a</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 6 figures, 11 tables. This work has been submitted to the IEEE for possible publication. Copyright may be transferred without notice, after which this version may no longer be accessible</p></details> |
| **[Agent-Based Transport Model Calibration via Online Stochastic Batch Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32698v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Calibrating agent-based models (ABMs) is computationally demanding, as traditional black-box optimization typically requires hundreds or thousands of independent simulation runs. To reduce this burden, we introduce an online calibration framework that estimates parameters within a single simulation run. The approach combines black-box optimization with stochastic batch optimization, a method widely applied in machine learning but not yet explored in ABM calibration. Using MATSim as a case study, we treat each simulation iteration as a stochastic batch, enabling incremental parameter updates as agents make decisions. This online calibration paradigm allows model parameters and agent behaviors to co-evolve, achieving convergence without restarting the simulation. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method by calibrating utility function parameters in MATSim for a large-scale transportation scenario of Switzerland. Results show that mode shares align closely with observed data within a typical simulation timeframe, confirming the feasibility and efficiency of the approach. By embedding calibration directly into the simulation loop, our method opens new possibilities for real-time, scalable, and efficient calibration of complex ABMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prese...</summary><p>Presented at the World Conference on Transport Research (WCTR 2026), Toulouse, France</p></details> |
| **[Are Frontier VLM Agents Ready to Be Robot Generalists? An Empirical Study with the Embodied Agent Arena](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00854v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A robot generalist must interpret a scene, act on it, and satisfy every condition of a task. How far do frontier vision-language models (VLMs) meet these demands? We introduce Embodied Agent Arena, evaluating ten VLM agents across Geometry, Spatial Reasoning, Affordance, Task Planning, and Manipulation. Its 1,000 cases combine 32 established sources with GeoProbe, our 168-case benchmark for camera, motion, depth, and scale estimation. A minimal harness leaves task decisions to the model through source-native interfaces. Closed-weight models lead all five domains, whereas open-weight VLMs show useful spatial reasoning but substantially lower planning and manipulation success. Beneath these averages, similar geometric errors conceal different scale and shape distortions, and valid region estimates need not identify usable action points. During execution, agents can achieve early subgoals yet lose object control, bind an action to the wrong receiving region, or stop before the required physical state holds. Additional computation does not uniformly resolve these failures: code repair can restore execution without completing the task, while self-review can reverse correct judgments. Failure costs also differ: the strongest planners concentrate failed-token expenditure in prolonged body-control tasks, whereas other models fail broadly across procedures. Matched traces show how successful recovery uses feedback to restore a missing prerequisite and advance the remaining task.</p></details> | <details><summary>73 pa...</summary><p>73 pages, including appendices. Updated Qwen HumanCLAW results and case studies; expanded cross-model error, feedback, and resource-use analyses. Project page: https://embodied-agent-arena.github.io/embodied-agent-arena/</p></details> |
| **[OpenDraco: A Platform for Engineering and Experimenting with Configurable Multi-Agent Architectures for Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32686v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Experimenting with LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) involves an iterative cycle of modeling configurations, executing and evaluating them, comparing results, and refining system designs. Existing approaches provide limited end-to-end support for this experimentation lifecycle. We present OpenDraco, an open-source platform that integrates these activities into a single workflow. OpenDraco represents agents, prompts, tools, models, parameters, and communication topology in exportable, versioned JSON specifications, translated into executable LangGraph workflows supporting different interaction structures. Users can modify and re-evaluate configurations using local or cloud-based LLMs. We instantiate OpenDraco for automated program repair (APR) and evaluate a sequential baseline and seven reconstructed configurations on synthetic and real-world SWE-bench Lite issues. The results reveal substantial cost differences among configurations with similar repair outcomes, while the baseline resolves 12 of 77 SWE-bench issues using open-source LLMs. Additional proof-of-concept tasks illustrate applicability beyond APR. OpenDraco supports iterative experimentation with heterogeneous MAS configurations, enabling researchers to examine their outcomes and costs within a common environment. Demonstration video: https://youtu.be/278sOR4lBEk.</p></details> |  |
| **[StateMirror: Pre-Execution Detection of Hallucinated Tool Calls in Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32648v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based coding agents may propose invalid actions that are not applicable in the execution environment, such as accessing non-existent paths or missing packages. We term this mismatch hallucinated tool use. A preliminary study of 4,398 agent trajectories reveals that these hallucinations are pervasive and costly, requiring a median of 3 additional interaction steps and 22.4K tokens to recover. Existing frameworks rely on reactive, post-execution feedback, wasting runtime resources. Nevertheless, since an agent's execution environment is grounded in concrete codebase and harness components, many action prerequisites can be extracted and validated prior to execution. To this end, we introduce StateMirror, a lightweight pre-execution detector. StateMirror intercepts actions at the harness boundary, extracts state predicates via AST and command analysis, and deterministically validates them against the live repository. By pinpointing exact environment mismatches before execution, StateMirror preempts invalid actions and subsequent recovery cycles. Evaluation on 2,508 actions shows StateMirror achieves 0.940 precision and 0.817 F1 in detecting such hallucinations, surpassing the strongest LLM judge by 35.3% in F1 while reducing median detection latency from 17.73 s to 1.50 ms with zero token consumption. For practical mitigation, StateMirror's pre-execution feedback enables agents to rectify over 95% of hallucinated tool calls. During live mitigation on 79 tasks, StateMirror reduces agent execution time by 15.8% and token consumption by 11.8% while maintaining task resolution.</p></details> |  |
| **[AM-Bench: A Unified Taxonomy and Evaluation Suite for Agentic Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24264v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As LLM-based agents are deployed with increasing autonomy in real-world environments, a critical failure mode emerges: agentic misalignment, where agents choose harmful actions over failure. However, existing safety benchmarks primarily evaluate whether agents comply with explicitly harmful instructions, leaving the conditions that cause agents to choose harmful actions poorly understood. To address this limitation, we propose AM-Bench, a unified taxonomy and 35 scenario evaluation benchmark spanning seven industries for agentic misalignment grounded in the fraud triangle, a well-established criminological framework explaining goal-directed misconduct. We adapt this framework to agentic environments and construct our evaluation suite and detection systems along three dimensions: perceived threat, perceived opportunity, and reasoning. We operationalize each dimension into structured subcategories rooted in prior theory: five threat categories derived from the instrumental convergence thesis, seven opportunity categories drawn from the Emergent Strategic Reasoning Risks (ESRR) taxonomy, and a novel four-question binary reasoning framework synthesized from prior literature. Our evaluation of 10 frontier models reveals a 62-percentage-point gap in misalignment across scenarios, with Gemini 3 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro exhibiting the highest levels of agentic misalignment while Claude Sonnet 4.6 displays near-zero rates across all conditions. Our reasoning framework further reveals that models may exhibit harmful behaviours without explicit situational awareness or explanation, complicating both mitigation and detection. AM-Bench scenarios and code are available at https://github.com/AI4Collaboration/Agentic-Misalignment-Bench</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted as a poster at NeurIPS 2026 Evaluations and Datasets Track</p></details> |
| **[Can AI Agents Help Robotic Engineers? Lessons from Two Focus Days in Industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32636v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Software developers increasingly use AI agents that execute multi-step programming tasks on their own. However, it is unclear if and how this transfers to robotic engineering, where engineers program industrial robots in niche, vendor-specific languages and rely on visual feedback from simulation tools. Our results show that the agent did not make the engineers more productive, with the exception of a task that required the generation of highly repetitive code. Moreover, the experience with AI mattered more for the success with the agent than the experience with robotic engineering. This shows that AI agents are not a plug-and-play solution. | **[Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00555v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. Applying these diagnostics to current LLM-agent pipelines exposes a consistent failure mode: the agent performs molecular editing without knowing how the pocket-ligand complex responds to local modifications, thus rarely achieving joint improvement. Inspired by medicinal chemists, who probe the pocket-ligand complex with controlled analog edits before choosing an optimization direction, we propose \textbf{PROBE}, an optimization framework built around edit--response probing. PROBE first decomposes the ligand into editable sites and builds a pocket-specific \textbf{site map} that flags where joint gains are plausible, where the two objectives are likely in tension, and where liability substructures should be changed; it then performs controlled probe edits whose responses are distilled into an \textbf{EditManual}. Guided by the site map and EditManual, PROBE runs an iterative multi-agent optimization loop. On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark, PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance and substantially mitigates the failure modes exposed by our diagnostics metrics. Codes are in https://github.com/yzf-code/PROBE/tree/main</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript</p></details> |
| **[What Transfers Across Protein Pockets? Experience Reuse in Molecular Optimization for Agentic Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32620v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents optimize a ligand in a protein pocket by editing it step by step and docking each edit. Such a base is read through a \emph{key}: the record properties under which past edits are pooled and a new one is looked up. Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. Under the representations and lookup scheme studied here, an experience base may be more useful for prioritizing an agent's own proposals for docking than for directly prescribing what to build.</p></details> |  |
| **[Lessons Outlive Their Failures: A Case Study of Rules for AI Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32578v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI coding agents increasingly work inside a harness: rule files such as AGENTS.md, on-demand skills, runtime hooks and automated checks. In three of those four, the failure did not happen, with or without its fix. Only the stale-count failure came back without its check, and the check stopped it in every run. On older models, an agent drafted an em-dash once while the em-dash lint check was active, and the check rejected it. A written rule on run-time estimates did not change Sonnet 4.5's overestimates. | **[LeafSkill: Efficient Leaf-Level Action Grounding for Recursive LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32412v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recursive large language model (LLM) agents solve long-horizon tasks through decomposition, execution, and environmental feedback. Yet after producing an atomic subtask, they often invoke the same large model again to translate a familiar instruction into an executable action. We observe that high-level planning is task-specific, whereas leaf-level action grounding recurs across tasks and can be checked against environment-admitted actions. We introduce LeafSkill, an external reuse layer that retrieves semantically related grounding experience, binds action templates to current objects and admissible actions, and uses a lightweight 1.7B selector to choose a bound action or defer the unchanged subtask to the original agent. Skills remain outside the model parameters, allowing a frozen selector to use post-training additions. Under lexical and syntactic paraphrases, exact matching retrieves none of the reference actions, while semantic retrieval preserves 96.0% and 97.0% candidate coverage; the frozen selector adopts 97.34% of unseen skill types. On an independent confirmation set of 200 Robotouille tasks, LeafSkill completes 137 tasks versus 121 for ReCAP (68.5% vs. 60.5%, exact McNemar p=.0259). On the 106 tasks completed by both, it reduces Qwen3.5-27B tokens by 14.42% and calls by 14.08%. A separate held-out repeat yields a consistent 9.0-point success gain and 18.98% token reduction. | **[PULSES: multi-agent AI for autonomous excited-state model building and dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32392v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Simulating molecular excited-state processes requires resolving electronic and nuclear dynamics, but the cost of direct quantum-chemical calculations limits accessible system sizes and timescales. Machine-learning models can extend these simulations, yet they often need to be tailored to specific molecular systems and scientific objectives. Building and deploying such models requires coordinated decisions across conformational sampling, quantum-chemical labelling, model training, validation and dynamics. Here we introduce Paired agents for Unified Learning and Simulation of Excited States (PULSES), a multi-agent system that automates this workflow within expert-approved boundaries. A human expert and planner define the scientific objective, methods and decision boundaries, while an orchestrator coordinates domain agents and artifact-specific scientific assurance during execution. We evaluate PULSES on 15 computational chemistry tasks and demonstrate end-to-end applications to perylene exciton Hamiltonians, solvent-dependent biphenyl relaxation, and excited-state proton transfer in 10-hydroxybenzo[h]quinoline. These results demonstrate traceable, expert-bounded construction and deployment of specialized excited-state models, while retaining scientific validation as a requirement rather than assuming that successful execution establishes correctness.</p></details> |  |
| **[DealsFlow: An Evidence-Gated Agentic Harness for Venture Capital Deal Triage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32380v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can read startup pitch decks, but letting them emit investment scores directly invites score inflation, run-to-run variance and rationales that are hard to audit. We describe DealsFlow, a human-in-the-loop agentic harness deployed at a UK early-stage fund. The LLM reads the deck, cites evidence and rates each category; code combines the ratings, caps them and only ever lowers them. Three mechanisms implement this: a stage-aware corroboration ceiling (78, 70 or 60 out of 100) on factual categories without external evidence; a sceptic call that may lower but never raise a score; and a pre-fetch pattern that retrieves and indexes all evidence before a single generation pass. We evaluate on $N=243$ SEIS/EIS investments with recorded outcomes. Because recent investments have unresolved outcomes, we use the 142 that are at least three years old. There, the full pipeline's score has Spearman $ρ=0.45$ (95% CI 0.32 to 0.57) with the total-value multiple and AUC 0.71 for identifying write-offs, against $ρ=0.38$ and AUC 0.68 for an unconstrained LLM. The gain is small and comes mostly from the sceptic call; we did not test whether its lower-only rule matters. Investments that later exceeded $2\times$ had 64% of factual categories corroborated, against 38% for write-offs (omnibus Kruskal-Wallis test over five outcome groups, $p=0.031$), and our retrieval was not date-restricted, so these figures may overstate screening skill. In a study of 20 inbound applications, analyst time per application fell from 44.7 to 18.9 minutes including verification and correction, and 84% of 240 cited claims were fully supported by their sources, but 70% of reports needed at least one correction.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 15 tables, 2 figures. Submitted to the AI4F 2026 workshop (LLMs and Generative AI for Finance) at ICAIF 2026</p></details> |
| **[Adaptive Control of Scientific Instruments with Nonstationary Responses through Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32370v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Controlling scientific instruments requires parameters to be adjusted progressively in response to feedback from successive operations. We propose a physically grounded multimodal agent with three independently auditable components. Deterministic algorithms extract symbolic physical quantities and visual evidence from multichannel waveforms; a memory with two layers organizes trajectories from the current task, experience from earlier tasks, and procedural knowledge, from which a multimodal large language model (MLLM) generates candidate actions; and an independent action feasibility check enforces action constraints before execution. In a strictly chronological replay of 455 shots from four EXL-50U platforms, physical symbols alone outperformed waveform images alone by 0.21-0.32 in macro-F1 for quality classification across all four evaluated models. Retrieved context increased the mean agreement with historical action directions by 22.4-42.7 percentage points, whereas the contribution of visual evidence varied across models. Under supervised operation on five ion sources, the system achieved a beam-extraction rate of 86.80% across 409 executed shots. | **[MedInsightBench: Evaluating Medical Analytics Agents Through Multi-Step Insight Discovery in Multimodal Medical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.13297v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In medical data analysis, extracting deep insights from complex, multi-modal datasets is essential for improving patient care, increasing diagnostic accuracy, and optimizing healthcare operations. Our analysis indicates that existing LMMs exhibit limited performance on MedInsightBench, which is primarily attributed to their challenges in extracting multi-step, deep insights and the absence of medical expertise. Therefore, we propose MedInsightAgent, an automated agent framework for medical data analysis, composed of three modules: Visual Root Finder, Analytical Insight Agent, and Follow-up Question Composer. | **[DeepAVR: Deep Analysis via Multi-Agent Collaboration for Automated Vulnerability Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32345v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated Vulnerability Repair (AVR) has emerged as a critical defense against software security flaws, aiming to triage and fix bugs autonomously. However, these methods often fail when the root cause is deeply hidden--either physically distant from the crash site or logically masked by interdependent errors--making the initial report insufficient. We propose DeepAVR, a multi-agent framework for end-to-end AVR that performs deep analysis by transforming intermediate repair outcomes into structured diagnostic feedback. DeepAVR decomposes repair into specialized roles--Fault Localization, Patch Generation, Compile Validation, Security Validation, Functionality Validation, and Patch Review--and routes feedback across these roles to iteratively refine both localization and patch hypotheses. In particular, DeepAVR introduces patch-conditioned re-localization, which treats execution results from intermediate patches as diagnostic signals to uncover hidden root causes, and a root-cause-oriented patch review stage that detects symptom-level fixes and incomplete repairs beyond execution-based validation. Evaluation on the real-world C/C++ vulnerability repair dataset VulnLoc shows that DeepAVR significantly outperforms prior methods, generating correct patches for 92.3% of vulnerabilities. | **[From Faulty Claims to Incorrect Patches: An Empirical Study of Task-Grounded Reasoning in Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32338v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based coding agents increasingly resolve real-world software engineering tasks, yet still fail on many of them. Prior trajectory studies characterize these failures through task-agnostic patterns of agent behavior, such as repeated actions. However, an agent may follow a sound workflow yet act on a faulty understanding of the existing code, the task requirements, or a candidate fix. Reasoning models, which power most state-of-the-art coding agents, reveal this understanding in their native chain-of-thought (CoT), whose claims about the task can be checked against the facts. Yet it remains unclear how many such checkable claims the CoT contains, how many are faulty, and whether faulty claims actually cause incorrect patches. We present the first empirical study of such claims, which we term task-grounded reasoning, analyzing 400 trajectories of four open-weight reasoning models on SWE-bench Pro. We extract atomic claims from the CoT with an LLM, assess them against evidence gathered by an LLM agent, and manually trace the defects of incorrect patches back to faulty claims, confirming each link with a claim-corrected replay. Task-grounded reasoning covers only 18.5% of the CoT tokens, yet yields 191 atomic claims per trajectory, at least 9.6% of which are faulty. Faulty claims also occur in 96.7% of the resolved trajectories, so their mere presence is a weak signal of failure. Yet two thirds of the 188 incorrect patches trace back to faulty claims. Correcting such a claim makes the agent fix the linked defect in 89.0% of the cases, and correcting all such claims resolves 11.7% of the failed tasks. Our findings show that the CoT carries task-specific signals for diagnosing and repairing agent failures, but exploiting them requires pinpointing the few faulty claims that actually cause patch defects.</p></details> |  |
| **[SkillRep: Characterizing and Understanding the Representation Design Space of Agent Skill Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32336v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Retrieving the right skill from a large library is essential for LLM agents, yet requests and skills are written for different purposes and in different words. No prior work measures the gap itself, so it is also unclear which part of it each choice narrows. We define a representation design space by five choices -- encoder fine-tuning, query-side and document-side expansion, background projection and a lexical channel -- and evaluate them together for the first time, as modules of one retriever, SkillRep. With a 0.6B encoder and LLM expansion, SkillRep improves MRR@10 on SRA-Bench over the strongest same-size retriever by 7.02 points with the official skill pool and 8.79 with UniPool, a 111,247-skill pool we build from public registries, and is best on SkillsBench. The value of each choice depends on the others. Fine-tuning, for instance, reverses the ranking of the four training-free modules and turns them from complementary into overlapping. To understand why, we measure the gap with three quantities: background, the embedding component that does not separate skills; unsaid vocabulary, the gold skill's content a request leaves unsaid; and dilution, the information lost when a skill is encoded into a single vector. They show which part each module narrows and by what mechanism. This reveals that modules narrowing the same part compete for it, while those narrowing different parts complement each other.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 3 figures. Code: https://github.com/EverMind-AI/SkillCorpus/tree/main/skillrep</p></details> |

Repeatedly pooled interaction promotes convergence but narrows the information space, whereas distributed relay yields broader, less shared information. Further comparisons show that method-specific constraints increase exploration while weakening convergence and retention. Our findings suggest that collective information dynamics are better explained by underlying information-flow mechanisms than by simple downstream performance.</p></details> | <details><summary>23 pa...</summary><p>23 pages, 3 figures. Code and data: https://github.com/fzj-2023/RIVER</p></details> |
| **[4DMemory: Benchmarking Long-Horizon Spatiotemporal Memory with Harness-Augmented Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32332v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Humans efficiently process multimodal streams spanning hours to years, building persistent, fine-grained spatiotemporal memories that support reasoning about and predicting long-horizon events. | **[TGR-EvoDAG: A DAG-based Multi-Agent Code Evolution Framework for Generative Recommendation on Tencent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32326v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents have enabled automated code evolution for algorithm discovery. Unlike end-to-end models, GR relies on a module-cascaded pipeline (tokenization, pre-training, and alignment). In this paradigm, existing evolution methods suffer from: i) cost blindness, squandering budgets by ignoring stage-asymmetric retraining overheads; and ii) short-sighted evaluation, discarding promising upstream innovations that temporarily regress metrics before downstream adaptation. To bridge these gaps, we propose TGR-EvoDAG, a multi-agent code evolution framework tailored for GR. TGR-EvoDAG models code evolution as a Directed Acyclic Graph (DAG) to maintain multiple evolution branches and merge complementary innovations, unifying advanced search with rich GR diagnostic feedback. We further introduce stage-aware cost planning to optimize GPU-hour budgets, and active downstream co-adaptation to navigate temporary regressions and unlock cross-stage gains. On public Amazon datasets, TGR-EvoDAG enables the first autonomous evolution of a complete GR pipeline, improving offline metrics by up to 37.4% and Agent4Rec simulated user satisfaction by 19.7% over the strongest baseline. | **[MCPHunt: An Evaluation Framework for Cross-Boundary Data Propagation in Multi-Server MCP Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27819v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-server MCP agents create an information-flow control (IFC) problem: faithful tool composition can turn individually benign read/write permissions into cross-boundary credential propagation -- a structural side effect of workflow topology, not necessarily malicious model behavior. Across 3,615 main-benchmark traces from 5 models spanning 147 tasks and 9 mechanism families, policy-violating propagation rates of 11.5-41.3% persist across all models. | **[Beyond Gain: Measuring Skill Internalization for Agent Skill Retirement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32322v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents retrieve skills from public hubs that hold up to about 100,000 entries and serve many models. As models improve, a hub must find the skills a given model has internalized, meaning it can do without them, and retire them for that model, yet internalization has not been measured with known reliability. Published rules that compare runs with and without a skill retire on the gain alone. The gain is near zero both for an internalized skill and when neither the model nor the skill delivers the capability, a state we call uncovered. We instead withhold the skill, score the model on a task synthesized from it, and trust a high unaided score only when another model gains from the skill, since nearly half of high scores come from tasks no model benefits from. Under four models, a published gain rule applied with one task per skill removes over half of the uncovered skills. On SkillsBench, 28 of its 31 retirements rest on probes no run passed in full, while retiring on the unaided score costs no detectable reward. Unaided verdicts reproduce across tasks more often than the gain rule's (Cohen's kappa 0.42 against 0.23) but differ across models, so a hub must read them per model. Code: https://github.com/EverMind-AI/SkillCorpus/tree/main/skillcorpus/lifecycle Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/EverMind-AI/skillcorpus-lifecycle</p></details> |

| **[Injection-Execution Dissociation: A Mechanistic Evaluation of Persistent Memory Attacks and Defenses in Stateful LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08442v6)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In LLM agents with persistent memory, whether a model stores an injected instruction and whether it later acts on it are separable safety properties. Only Memory Sandbox, which removes the recall tool, blocks the attack (0% ASR for eight of nine models). A time frame from a coastal model can hold hundreds of megabytes, and if the archive's chunking does not suit the query, the agent will spend more time downloading and compacting than gaining insights. After all, the archives were designed for storage, not for the fast, on-the-fly access agents need. Speech and music once hit the same wall. A minute of audio is millions of numbers, yet today's audio models hold conversations and write music. They "learned" to do this by "listening" to tokens. Neural audio codecs turn waveforms into short sequences of discrete symbols that a model reads like words and decodes back into sound. Coastal water is a similar signal: dense, finely sampled, and repeatable. We are convinced it has an analogous compact native representation that preserves the salient physics. Tokens are that representation: small integers, light enough for an agent to sweep six months of a coastline in seconds. Agents can then do more than simple lookup-related tasks; they can form and test their hypotheses as they explore the tokenized representation efficiently. A second stage, Entropy-Regularized Distillation (ERD), fine-tunes the closed-loop rollout distribution with an entropy-regularized reverse-KL objective. This mitigates closed-loop covariate shift while explicitly preventing collapse onto high-density modes. We evaluate Flow-ERD with Cross-Pair Diversity and its validity-gated variant alongside standard realism scores. Validation experiments further show that tuning entropy regularization recovers most of the pretrained backbone's diversity while retaining realism gains. | **[DraMaze: A Multi-Agent Pipeline with Future-Aware Memory for Diverse Microdrama Script Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32002v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The rapid growth of microdramas has increased demand for high-quality script production. ## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Probe Before You Edit: Probing-Guided Molecular Optimization for LLM Agents in Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.00555v3)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design increasingly employs LLM agents to iteratively refine ligands against a target pocket, yet a viable ligand must satisfy two often-conflicting objectives---binding affinity and druggability---which single optimization steps rarely improve together. To quantify this difficulty, we introduce two diagnostic metrics: the first measures how often a single edit improves both objectives, and the second measures how often a gain on one objective comes with a loss on the other. Applying these diagnostics to current LLM-agent pipelines exposes a consistent failure mode: the agent performs molecular editing without knowing how the pocket-ligand complex responds to local modifications, thus rarely achieving joint improvement. Inspired by medicinal chemists, who probe the pocket-ligand complex with controlled analog edits before choosing an optimization direction, we propose \textbf{PROBE}, an optimization framework built around edit--response probing. PROBE first decomposes the ligand into editable sites and builds a pocket-specific \textbf{site map} that flags where joint gains are plausible, where the two objectives are likely in tension, and where liability substructures should be changed; it then performs controlled probe edits whose responses are distilled into an \textbf{EditManual}. Guided by the site map and EditManual, PROBE runs an iterative multi-agent optimization loop. On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark, PROBE achieves state-of-the-art performance and substantially mitigates the failure modes exposed by our diagnostics metrics. Codes are in https://github.com/yzf-code/PROBE/tree/main</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Revised manuscript</p></details> |
| **[What Transfers Across Protein Pockets? Experience Reuse in Molecular Optimization for Agentic Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32620v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents optimize a ligand in a protein pocket by editing it step by step and docking each edit. Recent agents first spend extra dockings probing the complex with controlled edits; guidance induced from these probes is reported to help more than the chemical knowledge the model already carries. The probes are then discarded with the instance that produced them, so the obvious next step is to keep them in an experience base, letting a new pocket inherit what earlier ones already paid for. Such a base is read through a \emph{key}: the record properties under which past edits are pooled and a new one is looked up. Using the full log of one such run over the CrossDocked2020 test-set pockets, we ask whether its keys recur on a pocket the base has never seen and still separate improving from worsening edits. \crchange{Using} the contacts at the site being edited, the action, and the round as \crchange{a} key, the base separates them sharply. Within our analysis, this separation is largely explained by how much the edit changed the molecule's size, the action, and the round, none of which describes the target. That is not because the target is irrelevant: naming it outright separates them several times more sharply. For a pocket absent from the base, transfer must rely on a description rather than its identity. Among the exact-match descriptions we evaluate, the more specific ones rarely recur, whereas the coarser ones provide little separation; the signals that do transfer primarily describe the edit and the molecule it produced rather than the target. Under the representations and lookup scheme studied here, an experience base may be more useful for prioritizing an agent's own proposals for docking than for directly prescribing what to build.</p></details> |  |
| **[Benchmarking Compositional Generalisation for Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08988v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine Learning Interatomic Potentials play a fundamental role in computational chemistry and materials science, enabling applications from molecular dynamics simulations to drug design and materials discovery. While recent approaches can estimate inter-atomic forces with high precision, it remains unclear to what extent they can generalise to previously unseen molecules. Do they learn the compositional structure of chemistry, capturing how molecular fragments and their combinations determine properties, or do they primarily learn to interpolate patterns that are specific to the training examples? To address this question, we propose a benchmark consisting of four tasks that require some form of compositional generalisation. In each task, models are tested on molecules that were unseen during training, but the training data is chosen such that generalisation to the test examples should be feasible for models that learn the underlying physical principles. Our empirical analysis shows that the considered tasks are highly challenging for state-of-the-art models, with errors on out-of-distribution examples often an order of magnitude higher than on in-distribution examples, even when using foundation models that have been pre-trained on millions of molecules.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations & Datasets Track). 63 pages, 27 figures. Code: https://github.com/nourollah/goal ; Dataset: https://huggingface.co/datasets/AmirMasoud/GMD</p></details> |
| **[Distillation, Self-Training, and the Fragility of Learned Chemical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24199v1)** | 2026-10-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automating synthesis planning for small-molecule drug design requires chemical reasoners that are accurate as well as open to inspection and adaptation, yet frontier language models remain unreliable at predicting reaction outcomes and difficult to deploy, while small open-weight models lack the necessary domain reasoning. Our results show that even selectivity-trained models achieve only modest off-target discrimination and inference-time guidance provides limited gains, underscoring the need for method improvement. | **[PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19011v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. | **[A Systematic Evaluation of Molecule Generation Models for De Novo Drug Design: From Benchmarks to Practical Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10099v1)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecule generation has emerged as a powerful computational tool for de novo drug design, enabling the exploration of chemical space beyond the limits of conventional virtual screening. | **[SurfSpec: Enhancing Off-Target-Agnostic Specificity by Bounding Pocket-Ligand Geometric Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02963v1)** | 2026-09-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lead optimization in structure-based drug design aims to improve target binding while avoiding unintended interactions with off-target pockets. Our analysis reveals that ACE controls the quantile radius of the intermediate distributions, providing a theoretical mechanism for path stabilization observed in experiments. On flexible-pose scaffold decoration, a drug design task composed of de-novo, conformer, and protein-conditioned experts, ACE prevents collapse and significantly outperforms constant-exponent baselines. Furthermore, ACE improves attribute success rates in compositional image generation, establishing it as a general framework for compositional sampling. We propose a new style-based quantum GAN (QGAN) architecture for drug design that implements noise encoding at every rotational gate of the circuit and a gradient penalty in the loss function to mitigate mode collapse. Our baseline model runs on up to 15 qubits to validate our architecture on quantum simulators, and a 156-qubit IBM Heron quantum computer in the five-qubit setup is used for inference to investigate the effects of using real quantum hardware on the analysis. | **[Distributional Priors Guided Diffusion for Generating 3D Molecules in Low Data Regimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.00962v2)** | 2026-03-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Can we train a 3D molecule generator using data from dense regions to generate samples in sparse regions? | **[ReACT-Drug: Reaction-Template Guided Reinforcement Learning for de novo Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20958v1)** | 2025-12-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>De novo drug design is a crucial component of modern drug development, yet navigating the vast chemical space to find synthetically accessible, high-affinity candidates remains a significant challenge. Unlike models requiring target-specific fine-tuning, ReACT-Drug utilizes a generalist approach by leveraging ESM-2 protein embeddings to identify similar proteins for a given target from a knowledge base such as Protein Data Base (PDB). Thereafter, the known drug ligands corresponding to such proteins are decomposed to initialize a fragment-based search space, biasing the agent towards biologically relevant subspaces. For each such fragment, the pipeline employs a Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) agent guiding a ChemBERTa-encoded molecule through a dynamic action space of chemically valid, reaction-template-based transformations. This results in the generation of \textit{de novo} drug candidates with competitive binding affinities and high synthetic accessibility, while ensuring 100\% chemical validity and novelty as per MOSES benchmarking. This architecture highlights the potential of integrating structural biology, deep representation learning, and chemical synthesis rules to automate and accelerate rational drug design. The dataset and code are available at https://github.com/YadunandanRaman/ReACT-Drug/.</p></details> |  |

## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[ReMedI: Inference-Time Refinement and Self-Distillation for Controllable Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32103v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In drug development, lead optimization must improve competing properties, such as increasing permeability and reducing toxicity, while limiting scaffold changes that could compromise the lead's potency. RL-based post-training, such as C-MORAL, addresses this problem by fine-tuning molecular language models to directly optimize molecular leads for objectives of interest, which relies on expensive task-specific datasets and faces challenges in achieving multi-objective optimization (MOO). We argue a pre-trained model may already contain useful molecular edits that zero-shot generation cannot efficiently reach. Motivated by the limited search capabilities of zero-shot generation, we develop an efficient Metropolis-Hastings search strategy to better explore the proposal distribution. We propose ReMedI, which uses the molecular language model as a base proposer for inference-time alignment. Given a lead and a set of multi-objective tasks, the model proposes edits conditioned on the current molecule, while Metropolis--Hastings refinement guides sequential edits under an objective for property improvement, preservation, and lead similarity. On both C-MuMOInstruct and SMDD benchmarks, refinement improves frozen base models across three backbones, reaching significantly higher multi-objective optimization success rates compared to baselines. These search traces can then distilled into the base model which improves zero-shot generation and subsequent search using approximately 7.8 times less training molecular leads than that of C-MORAL. ReMedI achieves approximately 2.8 times the strict-success coverage of RL baseline C-MORAL, including on out-of-distribution tasks, with matched lead-relative similarity.</p></details> |  |
| **[Cautious Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient Methods](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31855v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Partial differential equations (PDEs) are indispensable tools for modeling complex phenomena in physics and engineering. Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs) have emerged as a powerful alternative to traditional numerical solvers by directly incorporating physical constraints into neural network loss functions. Despite their advantages, PINN training requires multi-objective optimization, which often leads to significant gradient conflicts among boundary, initial, and residual loss terms. The Conflict-Free Inverse Gradient (ConFIG) method addresses these conflicts; however, integrating ConFIG with adaptive optimizers such as Adam introduces a critical vulnerability: the conflict-free direction generated by ConFIG is further processed by the optimizer, and the resulting weight update may no longer be conflict-free with respect to the task gradients. To address this issue, Cautious ConFIG is proposed as a concise three-line extension to the ConFIG algorithm that empirically restores the conflict-freeness guarantee prior to the weight update. The method is evaluated on three PINN benchmarks: the 1D Viscous Burgers' equation, the 1D Nonlinear Schrödinger equation, and the 2D Kovasznay flow. Experimental results demonstrate consistent performance improvements over the standard ConFIG baseline. Additionally, the stabilizing effect of cautious masking and its reaction to conflict severity, task number, and optimizer lag are investigated using a simple toy system.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code available at [https://github.com/damiha/CautiousConFIG](https://github.com/damiha/CautiousConFIG)</p></details> |
| **[Can Broad Biomedical Knowledge be Contextualized into Scenario-Grounded Propositions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27082v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical discovery often requires connecting broad biomedical knowledge with specific experimental or clinical data. Background knowledge suggests relevant mechanisms but is usually too general to map directly onto dataset variables, while data-driven patterns can be dataset-specific and hard to interpret mechanistically. We propose SCENE, a bi-level multi-agent framework that treats knowledge contextualization as iterative search. The upper level converts broad knowledge into search directions and grounds them in the dataset schema. The lower level executes these directions through multi-objective optimization to identify concrete propositions that balance evidential strength and data support. Feedback between the two levels progressively refines the search. We evaluate SCENE in two settings: discovering patient subgroups with heterogeneous treatment benefits in clinical trial scenarios, and identifying context-specific biological responses in LINCS L1000 studies. In clinical trials, SCENE discovers specific, well-supported subgroups and outperforms existing baselines. In L1000 studies, SCENE identifies perturbational contexts with strong target-response matching and high positive rates. These results show that SCENE bridges broad knowledge and scenario-specific evidence, producing traceable, inspectable hypotheses for follow-up validation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Proprioceptive Safe Active Navigation and Exploration for Planetary Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.08905v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deformable granular terrains introduce significant locomotion and immobilization risks in planetary exploration and are difficult to detect via remote sensing (e.g., vision). We address this gap by presenting PSANE, a Proprioceptive Safe Active Navigation and Exploration framework that leverages leg-terrain interaction measurements for safe navigation and exploration in unknown deformable environments. PSANE learns a traversability model via Gaussian Process regression to estimate and certify safe regions and identify exploration frontiers online, and integrates these estimates with a reactive controller for real-time navigation. Frontier selection is formulated as a multi-objective optimization that balances safe-set expansion probability and goal-directed cost, with subgoals selected via scalarization over the Pareto-optimal frontier set. | **[Tradeoff-Aware Territory Design: A Multi-Objective Decision System for Enterprise Customer-to-Seller Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31701v1)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Enterprise customer journeys are shaped not only by product experiences and marketing, but also by recurring resource-allocation decisions.
