It integrates three components: a multidimensional account-value layer that combines predictive signals to score opportunity; an observational continuity-valuation layer that estimates the cost of breaking seller-account relationships using a synthetic-control-style counterfactual framework; and a multi-objective optimization layer that allocates coverage to high-value accounts while considering continuity, opportunity balance, and operational constraints. In production planning, adopted scenarios improved both assignment compliance and opportunity balance relative to the previous baseline. Crucially, to support this curriculum, BIRD-RL is driven by a Trajectory-scoped Persistent Execution Infrastructure, which enables safe database replica reuse while preserving trajectory isolation. | **[Enabling Validation for Robust Few-Shot Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04713v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A key challenge in Few-Shot Recognition (FSR) is the scarcity of labeled task-specific data for training. ## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Data-Driven Soft Labeling Scales DNA Read Classification to Whole-Body Cell-Type Deconvolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04987v5)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cell-type deconvolution, the task of estimating the proportions of constituent cell types in a heterogeneous biological sample, is a core problem in computational biology. Methods that rely on epigenetic marks such as DNA methylation typically operate on aggregated methylation estimates, discarding the pattern-level information carried by individual DNA reads. Existing read-level approaches that exploit this information are scarce, and all remain restricted to few-class settings; scaling them further is an open problem because, at scale, non-discriminative reads dominate and hard labels conflict with the many-to-many mapping between methylation patterns and cell types, preventing classifier convergence. To overcome this, we propose data-driven soft labels that estimate the conditional cell-type distribution for each read, and integrate this scheme into $Syto$, a new modular framework for read-level classification-based deconvolution. On a whole-body atlas of 39 human cell types, $Syto$ reduces MSE by 3.7$\times$ over the best examined baseline, with gains transferring to an out-of-distribution dataset spanning 16 tissues. $Syto$ lays the foundation for modeling increasingly large cell-type panels, with improved applications in biology and healthcare. The proposed soft-labeling scheme is further translatable to any setting with a many-to-many signal-to-label mapping.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (Main Track), poster</p></details> |
| **[Secure Multimodal Federated Optimization via Hierarchical DNA-Based Key Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27644v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Federated optimization over heterogeneous multimodal data enables distributed institutions to collaboratively optimize a shared model using medical images, genomic profiles, clinical records, and physiological signals without centralizing raw data. This paper proposes a secure multimodal federated optimization framework that separates local data protection from federated-update protection. A trusted key-management authority securely provisions one client key through Hybrid Public Key Encryption (HPKE), from which each client locally derives modality-specific symmetric keys using the domain-separated HMAC-based Extract-and-Expand Key Derivation Function (HKDF). Heterogeneous modalities are mapped into a unified DNA-domain representation and processed through a DNA-origami-inspired structural transformation before authenticated encryption with AES-GCM. During local optimization, only the required modalities are decrypted within an authorized client environment. Individual model updates are independently protected through secure aggregation, allowing the coordinator to observe only aggregated updates rather than each client's contribution. Together, these components establish a structured approach to modality-specific protection and secure collaborative optimization, integrating hierarchical key management, authenticated local encryption, and aggregation-level update protection within a unified framework.</p></details> |  |

One hypothetical implementation can use CRISPR-Cas9 and gRNA reagents for labeling. Various aspects of the general labeling channel, including fixed-length labels, are explored, and upper bounds on the maximum size of the corresponding codes are given. This survey reviews experimentally validated DNA-tiles, nanocarriers, and biosensors relevant to implementing DNA-tile-based nanonetworks in vitro. In particular, for $t=1$ and $C>1/2$ we construct efficient codes with redundancy that essentially matches our non-constructive upper bound; b) $k$ equal a constant; in this case we consider the extremal problem where the number of deletions is not bounded and a deletion is imposed after every run of length at least $k$, which we call the extremal contextual deletion channel. This combinatorial setting arises naturally by considering a probabilistic channel that introduces contextual deletions after each run of length at least $k$ with probability $p$ and taking the limit $p\to 1$. | **[Measure Before You Train: Typed Decision Models vs. a Classical Baseline vs. a Generative LLM for Routing a Personal Multilingual Inbox](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32955v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Personal email agents decide, for every message, what it is, whether it is urgent, whether to draft a reply, create a task, or ignore it. Their error rate is rarely measured: the stored labels were written by the system itself. We present Foyer, a self-hosted personal inbox agent in daily use, and a measurement protocol (stratified sampling, frozen snapshots, thread-grouped splits, pre-registered hypotheses, human labels in the agent's review loop) that yields ground truth before any training. On 739 real Italian/English emails labelled by the owner (231 unbiased, 500 adversarially sampled), we compare nine systems under thread-grouped cross-validation: the production keyword rules, a TF-IDF + logistic-regression baseline, local typed decision models (Tev1, Nimble), a hosted decision model (Nimble v4-max), and hosted generative LLMs (Qwen2.5-7B, Gemma 4 26B/31B). Four results. The production rules score 28% on message type against a 58% majority class: the field shown most prominently carried no information. With ~200 labels, logistic regression has the highest accuracy on every field (type 0.79, urgency 0.74, needs-reply 0.83, is-noise 0.90), though it never predicts the two rarest classes and is statistically tied with Gemma 4 on type; few-shot prompting does not close the gap. The sender's relationship (client, supplier, service) is recovered from other emails of the same contact with 98.6% accuracy where the contact has been seen, and is not inferable from a single message by any model: it belongs in the contact graph, not the prompt. Finally, accuracy is not the product metric: composed into actions, the most accurate system most often ignores emails needing action (18% vs 4% for the best LLM); a cost-chosen threshold on its noise probability halves that rate and makes it the cheapest system.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 7 tables. Single-user case study. Code and protocol: https://gitlab.com/federicolucca/foyer-measure . The emails are private and not released</p></details> |
| **[VETTA: Coordinating Turn- and Token-Level Credit Assignment for Multi-Turn LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08402v2)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn LLM agents often receive sparse task feedback across several interactions, while generating each response token by token. This creates two related credit-assignment questions: which responses helped achieve the outcome, and which generation decisions mattered within each response? Existing methods typically focus on only one level: turn-level methods evaluate complete responses but do not distinguish the decisions within them; token-level methods can propagate feedback across turns but do not explicitly model credit for each response. These complementary limitations motivate learning credit at both levels and coordinating it in a single policy update. We introduce VETTA, a credit assignment method that jointly learns turn- and token-level values through separate heads on a shared lightweight critic. VETTA computes advantages along both temporal sequences and combines each turn advantage with a within-response-centered token residual for PPO updates. Furthermore, to reduce value-learning cost, the critic retains only early Transformer blocks from the pretrained checkpoint used to initialize the actor. Experiments on four agent benchmarks demonstrate VETTA's strong performance. With Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, VETTA achieves success rates of 95.5% on ALFWorld, 76.0% on WebShop, and 52.2% on ScienceWorld. With Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct, it reaches 84.9% and 80.7% success on Sokoban under sparse and dense rewards, respectively. Critic-depth comparisons further show strong task performance with substantially lower critic-side computation. These results demonstrate that coordinating turn- and token-level credit with a lightweight shared critic improves agent performance while keeping value estimation efficient. Code is available at https://github.com/Jiaju-Chen/VETTA-official.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 6 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Monetizing Intelligence: Advertising and Subscription Design on LLM Platforms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32945v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) platforms introducing advertising must decide how much capability ad revenue should fund and what consumers should pay to avoid ads. We study a monopolistic platform that jointly chooses costly response quality, advertising intensity, and subscription prices when advertising reduces user value but better service raises advertising returns. Three findings follow. First, when partly ad-supported and ad-free plans coexist, advertising can finance useful service for consumers who receive none under pure subscription. Lower effective quality in middle plans weakens the alternatives available to premium users, allowing higher prices for unchanged ad-free service. Some fully ad-supported users gain utility, while partly ad-supported and ad-free users lose: broader access can coexist with utility polarization. Second, an intermediate advertising cap can maximize consumer surplus by preserving some revenue that finances service while limiting degradation. Third, cheaper compute can raise quality and reduce ad intensity, yet sufficiently large cost reductions can increase aggregate advertising revenue above its initial level because better service raises the value of the remaining advertised interactions. An extension shows how advertising-funded upgrades can bring plans to the same maximum capability and advertising exposure, eliminating the basis for different prices. These results make menu design central to evaluating LLM advertising: the consequences extend beyond users who see ads to the alternatives and prices faced by those who do not.</p></details> |  |
| **[Green Code or Costly Code? Evaluating the Energy Costs of Iterative LLM-Based Code Optimizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32891v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used to optimize code for performance. This practice often assumes that improvements in execution speed naturally translate into overall lower energy consumption. We challenge this assumption empirically by measuring whether performance-optimized code produced by LLM self-refinement pipelines is also energy-efficient relative to canonical human reference solutions, and whether the energy cost of the optimization process can be offset by subsequent execution-time savings. We evaluate 11 open-source models across four decoding configurations on all 1,000 problems of EffiBench, a state-of-the-art performance benchmark, while collecting per-process, hardware-level energy measurements throughout. Inference energy spans 0.79 to 8.68 MJ across model-variant pairs, which is roughly equivalent to the energy of 250 to 2800 GPT-4.1 queries. Limiting the maximum number of output tokens has the largest impact, reducing energy use by up to 69% compared to the default configuration. On the execution side, 42 of 44 configurations produce code that consumes more energy per problem than the canonical human reference, with overheads ranging from +4% to +886%. The two exceptions achieve only marginal gains, with break-even points on the order of 10^6 to 10^7 executions. Mixture-of-Experts architectures consume substantially less inference energy than equivalently-sized dense models without sacrificing correctness, making architectural choice a more consequential sustainability lever than decoding parameters. Performance optimization and energy efficiency are not always aligned goals: LLM self-refinement consistently produces faster-seeking but energy-heavier code relative to what a human engineer would write. All artifacts are available at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.23598170.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 49th IEEE/ACM International Conference on Software Engineering (ICSE 2027)</p></details> |

LEMS advances rank allocation by introducing a layer-wise error surrogate that integrates local and global layer importance alongside a propagation bias, enabling effective global rank allocation via an ILP formulation. Across Mistral, Qwen3, and Llama 3 model families, we show that LEMS consistently outperforms existing search strategies, delivering significant zero-shot accuracy gains of up to 4.8 p.p. that generalize to model sizes of 70B parameters, while KFAC-SVD achieves an average perplexity improvement of 15%. | **[Trusting the Tool: Asymmetric Deference of LLM Vulnerability Auditors to Static-Analysis Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32876v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pipelines that combine large language models (LLMs) with static analysis let the model consume the analyser's output as evidence, but whether the model distinguishes true from false evidence, and whether its scrutiny depends on what the evidence claims, has not been measured. The study covers 916 pairs in four disjoint sets, eleven model configurations from four vendors and 16,572 audits; no model is trained or fine-tuned. True evidence barely improved accuracy (+0.02). False evidence acted asymmetrically: relative to true notes about the same operations, false assurances lowered the detection of real vulnerabilities by up to 19 points, whereas false alarms never significantly lowered accuracy and were disputed far more often. An internally registered replication on seven new models confirmed that false assurances move verdicts towards their claim more than false alarms do (mean asymmetry +0.16, 95% CI +0.10 to +0.24). Instructions in the audit prompt did not reliably reduce deference; the best one failed to replicate. Verifying each claim in a separate call and showing the refuted claims raised accuracy under false assurances by 12 points in a pre-specified test (p=0.002) without loss under true evidence; for a second model the gain was smaller and not significant. Pipelines should therefore verify a tool's guarantees before the model relies on them, and studies should measure deference in a direction-aware way.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 9 figures, 5 tables. Submitted to IEEE Access. Code: https://github.com/lzh0082-cloud/CODE-DETECTION</p></details> |
| **[Do "True" and "False" Add Up to One? Complement Consistency in LLM-Agent Opinion Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32874v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In LLM-agent opinion simulations, each agent reports the probability that a claim is true after seeing its own previous probability and the probabilities reported by its neighbors, and that number becomes its state in the next round. The discrepancy is two to ten times larger than the change produced by a same-meaning paraphrase, and it persists under repeated sampling and across peer group sizes and report strengths. When the reports are fed back into two populations that start from complementary states, the populations do not remain mirror images: their divergence is positive after five rounds for every model. Averaging each answer with the complement of its paired answer restores consistency by construction and lowers Brier loss averaged over the two wordings, but at the final round it beats the better of the two original wordings for only one of the three models. A schedule-by-targeting experiment on the same model gives a small, stable interaction because such sweeps keep the claim wording fixed. Consistency under rewording therefore needs its own test before simulated probabilities are read as beliefs</p></details> |  |
| **[AI Alignment for Digital Democracy: Fairer Voters Representation against LLM Biases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32780v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI representation is emerging in digital democracy, assisting in voter emulation and decision-making. | **[Async-RBench: Benchmarking Asynchronous Task Execution in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32429v1)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute tasks asynchronously: a main agent continues working while its subagents run, and the task state may change before their results arrive. We make this exposure measurable by quantifying how accurately a target can be profiled, and how far that profile enables social engineering attacks. Because no existing model fully spans this continuum, we unify Social Media Intelligence (SOCMINT) and Cyber Human Intelligence (Cyber-HUMINT), two individual-focused disciplines that evolved separately, into a single doctrine-grounded taxonomy of 38 capabilities across the Cyber Threat Intelligence lifecycle, where user profiling bridges passive collection and active engagement. Domain experts validated the taxonomy (Fleiss' $κ$ = 0.92). Susceptibility is assessed indirectly: the pipeline predicts how each target would rank personalized engagement options, matching their actual choices significantly above chance. Overall, our results demonstrate that proactively engaging users through established human practices is beneficial for effective LLM-supported co-authoring, providing useful methodologies, techniques and insights for future research on collaborative agents.</p></details> |  |
| **[AgentMonopoly: Behavioral Patterns and Persona Control in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29330v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>How do LLM agents' strategies differ, and how much can instructions to adopt a persona change their behavior over a game? We study these questions with AgentMonopoly, an environment where agents communicate, buy and trade properties, and make decisions that affect later turns. We compare models from four families, each at two sizes, in games with identical models, different sizes of the same family, or different families. We also assign one agent a persona, a description of how it should behave, intended to change a tendency observed in games without that persona. The tested models differ in how often they trade, waive rent, and manage cash and properties. Larger models do not consistently win more games. Persona instructions change the words agents use and some of their actions, but the changes do not always match the role across all decisions. These results show why persona following should be assessed through actions and outcomes as well as language. As our findings are conditional on the tested checkpoints, prompts, and five seeds per configuration, the open-source nature of AgentMonopoly provides the experimental infrastructure for further extending these comparisons. Our code is available at https://github.com/SuELS-Group/Agent_Monopoly.</p></details> | <details><summary>14 pa...</summary><p>14 pages, 8 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[Investigating Multi-Cultural Value Selection Biases in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31199v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) serve users whose cultural identities are multi-layered, yet cultural evaluations commonly reduce identity to one label. In this work, we study LLMs under simultaneous identity cues, with a country persona in the system prompt and a second identity signaled in the user turn, either by naming a country (explicit cue) or through the query language (implicit cue). We test World Values Survey (WVS) multiple-choice probing and open-ended sociopolitical generation. In multiple-choice probing, language cues shift answers about three times more than explicit cues but away from the associated country's distribution, and explicit cues do not consistently move answers toward the named country. Across 1.6 million open-ended responses, naming a country closes 30-44% of the gap to the model's own responses for that country, and explicit cues produce more differentiated value changes than language cues. Our results suggest that country and language cues should be evaluated as distinct signals rather than interchangeable proxies for culture.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages, 13 figures, 18 tables</p></details> |
| **[Rethinking LLM-Guided Visual Question Answering: Does Deeper Knowledge Integration Really Help?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17079v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Some questions about an image need commonsense or factual knowledge that its pixels do not contain. Large Language Models (LLMs) can supply this knowledge to Visual Question Answering (VQA) systems, but the depth at which their guidance should enter a vision-language model remains unclear. We prompt the 70-billion-parameter Llama~3.3 model to describe, for each question, the visual concepts to focus on, the distractors to ignore and the external facts needed. We inject this guidance into a frozen Vision Transformer (ViT) paired with an instruction-tuned Text-to-Text Transfer Transformer (T5) in two ways: decision-level late fusion and attention-matrix injection. On the Outside Knowledge VQA (OK-VQA) benchmark, and contrary to the common view that deeper is better, the shallow route wins: late fusion reaches 39.4% exact match, +10.0 points above an unguided baseline, while attention injection reaches only 31.8% under data-scarce fine-tuning. An LLM paraphrasing pipeline also enlarges the training set more than tenfold. The results suggest that integration depth should be matched to the data regime.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 6 figures. Accepted at ADCIS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Prompt and Parser Engineering: Soft-Prompt Tuning for Automated LLM Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12117v2)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Benchmark scores often misrepresent a large language model's (LLM's) knowledge, because they rely, e.g., on the model's ability to follow specific formatting requirements. This especially penalizes base models that may know the correct answers but lack the ability -- typically introduced in post-training -- to structure them as instructed. To overcome this, we propose an efficient, automated, and architecture-agnostic method for model evaluation based on soft-prompt tuning. By optimizing only 10 soft-prompt vectors (~0.0006% parameters for a 7B model) over a short tuning period, we adapt models to specific benchmark formats, closing gaps in format-following and ensuring that underlying knowledge is accurately reflected in benchmark scores. We evaluate soft-prompt tuning across 7 models and 11 datasets. Our results show that (a) soft-prompt tuning saturates format-following within 150 steps (~1200 samples) while preserving knowledge accuracy, (b) it closely matches strong evaluation-harness baselines on well-studied benchmarks while requiring no manual benchmark-specific prompt or parser design, (c) it improves evaluation signal in settings where baselines are harder to transfer, including long-context datasets and instruction-tuned variants with different output styles, and (d) it produces cleaner outputs that improve agreement between exact match scoring and independent LLM judges. Overall, we propose a lightweight, automated, and flexible protocol based on limited-budget soft-prompt tuning for evaluating LLM knowledge.</p></details> | 9 pages, 7 figures |
| **[Prompting Strategy Effects Depend on the Judge: A Multilingual Study of LLM-as-a-Judge Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31172v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-as-a-Judge evaluation is increasingly applied to multilingual tasks, yet prompting strategies such as Chain-of-Thought and Role Conditioning are recommended based solely on English benchmarks. Whether their effectiveness transfers across judge models and languages remains unknown: prompt-sensitivity studies are largely English-centric, while existing multilingual judge studies fix the prompt template. In this work, we address this gap by measuring this interaction through a benchmark of 9 prompting strategies, 4 open-weight judges, 27 languages, and 4 tasks across 13 datasets, yielding over 1M pointwise scores on gold-reference outputs, validated against human judgments. We find that prompting strategies are not transferable: the choice of the judge model dominates the choice of prompting strategy, strategy effects reverse direction across judges, and stability degrades in low-resource languages, where these effects are concealed by aggregate reporting. These findings show that prompting effects in multilingual evaluation are conditioned on the judge, task, and language. Consequently, prompting strategies should be validated for the intended evaluation setting, and cross-study comparisons should account for both judge and prompt configuration.</p></details> | 23 pages, |
| **[SARIA: Persistence-Aware KV Caching with Coordinated DRAM--SSD Reload for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31169v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-turn LLM agents usually reuse long context prefixes, making key value (KV) caching crucial for reducing prefill latency. SSDs provide a low-cost extension to limited DRAM capacity but introduce two key challenges. Eagerly persisting all KV cache blocks incurs substantial write traffic and rapidly consumes finite SSD endurance. Meanwhile, the small number of SSDs provisioned in typical GPU servers limits aggregate reload bandwidth, making SSD-resident KV cache much slower to retrieve than that in DRAM and inflating prefill latency. We present \textsc{Saria} (Selective Admission and Reload for Inference Acceleration), a KV caching system that jointly manages DRAM and SSD by exploiting the session-level temporal locality of LLM agents to reduce SSD writes and accelerate cache reload. On the write path, \textsc{Saria} uses eviction and reuse history to selectively persist KV cache blocks that exhibit reuse opportunities beyond their DRAM residency, avoiding unnecessary writes for KV cache already hosted in DRAM. On the read path, \textsc{Saria} promotes selected SSD-hit KV cache blocks from GPU memory into DRAM after prefill, allowing subsequent reuse within the same session to reduce SSD read traffic while limiting DRAM contention. A transfer coordinator paces DRAM reads while SSD transfers are active, allowing both paths to make progress and efficiently share GPU ingress bandwidth. We implement \textsc{Saria} on LMCache MP and evaluate it using workloads from TraceLab and WEKA, covering both full-attention and hybrid-attention models. The results show that \textsc{Saria} reduces SSD writes by 57.1--58.9\% compared with popular full duplicate caching and the mean time to first token (TTFT) by 42.3--79.7\% over DRAM-only caching.</p></details> | 12 pages, 16 figures |
| **[Network topology shapes consensus and vulnerability in LLM populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31165v1)** | 2026-10-22 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models (LLMs) are deployed in interacting populations, their collective behaviour depends not only on the models but also on who interacts with whom. Studies of convention formation in LLM populations have mainly tested well-mixed interactions. We place policies extracted from four LLMs on fully connected, random regular, and heterogeneous networks, with $10^3$ to $10^5$ agents playing the naming game. Topology affects how quickly consensus emerges, sometimes whether it emerges at all, and, in rare cases, which convention wins. Sparsest networks slow consensus up to twelvefold. Yet, by preserving correlations between persistent neighbours, they restore consensus in some cases that fail to converge under well-mixed interactions. Networks also govern vulnerability to adversarial attack. We measure the critical mass of committed agents needed to overturn an established convention. Tipping thresholds follow the same ordering across models and conditions: hubs $\leq$ random $\leq$ low-degree nodes. On the most heterogeneous topology, median critical masses are 3%, 16%, and 35%, respectively; targeting hubs requires one-sixth as many committed agents as random placement. For most models, networked populations are easier to tip than well-mixed ones, so well-mixed estimates can overstate robustness. Assessing the safety of multi-agent AI requires mapping who interacts with whom.</p></details> |  |
